The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light, it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings made it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still take care of the essentials around the house, like cooking and keeping things clean. But... I have pulled back from a few things. I used to be very involved in a walking group, and I did some mentoring for younger women, but I just can't seem to follow through on those commitments right now. I don't feel like I can be fully present with people the way I used to be, so it's just easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Is your brother’s situation still ongoing right now?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure. I haven't really... I don't know exactly where things stand with him right now. I just haven't been asking about it lately, so I really couldn't say for certain.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, are your thoughts mostly about your brother?\n\nPatient: Yes, they often are. I... I just keep going back over his decline in my head. I tell myself that if I had been a better sister, or maybe just more attentive, I would have noticed what was happening sooner. I really feel like I could have prevented some of this if I had just paid attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I catch myself thinking things like... well, that it would just be simpler if I didn't wake up tomorrow. I want to be clear that I don't have any actual plan to harm myself or anything like that. But just the fact that my mind is going there at all was unsettling enough that I knew I needed to come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Most days, it just feels as if a gray film has settled over everything. Even when I’m just doing my normal routine, like making a cup of tea or looking out at my garden, there’s this... quiet heaviness in me. And I just can’t quite seem to reason my way out of it, no matter how hard I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way nearly every day for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been... well, I'd say about eight months. It really started right around the time my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after your brother’s health and behavior changed?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's right. Everything just seemed to shift right around that time, and I haven't been able to shake this feeling since.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes, thinking I'll paint. But... I'll just sit there staring at the paper, and eventually I end up putting it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light, it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings made it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still take care of the essentials around the house, like cooking and keeping things clean. But... I have pulled back from a few things. I used to be very involved in a walking group, and I did some mentoring for younger women, but I just can't seem to follow through on those commitments right now. I don't feel like I can be fully present with people the way I used to be, so it's just easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Is your brother’s situation still ongoing right now?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure. I haven't really... I don't know exactly where things stand with him right now. I just haven't been asking about it lately, so I really couldn't say for certain.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, are your thoughts mostly about your brother?\n\nPatient: Yes, they often are. I... I just keep going back over his decline in my head. I tell myself that if I had been a better sister, or maybe just more attentive, I would have noticed what was happening sooner. I really feel like I could have prevented some of this if I had just paid attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things in your life, not just your brother?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I worry about a lot of different things. It's really just... my mind always goes back to him. The rest of my life is fairly quiet right now, so I don't have a lot of other things I'm constantly worrying about. It's just that one main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any change there. I think my appetite has stayed pretty much the same. I'm still eating about as much as I usually do.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about your brother, does the heaviness lift at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Most days it just feels like... well, like a gray film has settled over everything. Even when I'm not actively dwelling on him, like if I'm just making tea or looking out at my garden, there's still this quiet heaviness in me. It's just always there in the background, and I can't quite reason my way out of it, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It's... well, getting to sleep is a bit uneven to begin with. But then I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. Once I'm awake, my mind just starts going over all the phone calls, the forms I need to deal with... just wondering if I handled things correctly. Because of all that, by the time morning actually comes, I feel like I've hardly rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration. I can still read my books and keep track of things around the house just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just keep going back over his decline in my head. I tell myself that if I had been a better sister, or maybe just more attentive, I would have noticed what was happening sooner. I really feel like I could have prevented some of this if I had just paid attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things in your life, not just your brother?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I worry about a lot of different things. It's really just... my mind always goes back to him. The rest of my life is fairly quiet right now, so I don't have a lot of other things I'm constantly worrying about. It's just that one main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any change there. I think my appetite has stayed pretty much the same. I'm still eating about as much as I usually do.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about your brother, does the heaviness lift at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Most days it just feels like... well, like a gray film has settled over everything. Even when I'm not actively dwelling on him, like if I'm just making tea or looking out at my garden, there's still this quiet heaviness in me. It's just always there in the background, and I can't quite reason my way out of it, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It's... well, getting to sleep is a bit uneven to begin with. But then I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. Once I'm awake, my mind just starts going over all the phone calls, the forms I need to deal with... just wondering if I handled things correctly. Because of all that, by the time morning actually comes, I feel like I've hardly rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration. I can still read my books and keep track of things around the house just fine.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've had sad days over the years, of course, just like anyone else. But never anything quite like this. This is really the first time in my life I've felt this sort of... constant heaviness for weeks and months on end. Before all this started, I was generally pretty content.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally scared me enough to make an appointment is that I've started having these troubling thoughts lately. I catch myself thinking things like... well, that it would just be simpler if I didn't wake up tomorrow. I want to be clear that I don't have any actual plan to harm myself or anything like that. But just the fact that my mind is going there at all was unsettling enough that I knew I needed to come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Most days, it just feels as if a gray film has settled over everything. Even when I’m just doing my normal routine, like making a cup of tea or looking out at my garden, there’s this... quiet heaviness in me. And I just can’t quite seem to reason my way out of it, no matter how hard I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way nearly every day for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, not two years. It’s been... well, I'd say about eight months. It really started right around the time my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after your brother’s health and behavior changed?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's right. Everything just seemed to shift right around that time, and I haven't been able to shake this feeling since.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes, thinking I'll paint. But... I'll just sit there staring at the paper, and eventually I end up putting it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light, it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings made it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still take care of the essentials around the house, like cooking and keeping things clean. But... I have pulled back from a few things. I used to be very involved in a walking group, and I did some mentoring for younger women, but I just can't seem to follow through on those commitments right now. I don't feel like I can be fully present with people the way I used to be, so it's just easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Is your brother’s situation still ongoing right now?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure. I haven't really... I don't know exactly where things stand with him right now. I just haven't been asking about it lately, so I really couldn't say for certain.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, are your thoughts mostly about your brother?\n\nPatient: Yes, they often are. I... I just keep going back over his decline in my head. I tell myself that if I had been a better sister, or maybe just more attentive, I would have noticed what was happening sooner. I really feel like I could have prevented some of this if I had just paid attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things in your life, not just your brother?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I worry about a lot of different things. It's really just... my mind always goes back to him. The rest of my life is fairly quiet right now, so I don't have a lot of other things I'm constantly worrying about. It's just that one main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any change there. I think my appetite has stayed pretty much the same. I'm still eating about as much as I usually do.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about your brother, does the heaviness lift at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It's just always there in the background, and I can't quite reason my way out of it, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It's... well, getting to sleep is a bit uneven to begin with. But then I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. Once I'm awake, my mind just starts going over all the phone calls, the forms I need to deal with... just wondering if I handled things correctly. Because of all that, by the time morning actually comes, I feel like I've hardly rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration. Before all this started, I was generally pretty content.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your sadness has been much stronger than you would have expected, given what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, losing my brother... it's a terrible thing, obviously. But I don't know if this is how I'm supposed to feel after something like that, or if it's... stronger than it should be. It's just hard to say what's normal in a situation like this.\n\nDoctor: When did your brother die?\n\nPatient: He passed away about eight months ago. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light, it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings made it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still take care of the essentials around the house, like cooking and keeping things clean. But... I have pulled back from a few things. I used to be very involved in a walking group, and I did some mentoring for younger women, but I just can't seem to follow through on those commitments right now. I don't feel like I can be fully present with people the way I used to be, so it's just easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Is your brother’s situation still ongoing right now?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure. I haven't really... I don't know exactly where things stand with him right now. I just haven't been asking about it lately, so I really couldn't say for certain.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, are your thoughts mostly about your brother?\n\nPatient: Yes, they often are. I... I just keep going back over his decline in my head. I tell myself that if I had been a better sister, or maybe just more attentive, I would have noticed what was happening sooner. I really feel like I could have prevented some of this if I had just paid attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things in your life, not just your brother?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I worry about a lot of different things. It's really just... my mind always goes back to him. The rest of my life is fairly quiet right now, so I don't have a lot of other things I'm constantly worrying about. It's just that one main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any change there. I think my appetite has stayed pretty much the same. I'm still eating about as much as I usually do.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about your brother, does the heaviness lift at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Most days it just feels like... well, like a gray film has settled over everything. Even when I'm not actively dwelling on him, like if I'm just making tea or looking out at my garden, there's still this quiet heaviness in me. It's just always there in the background, and I can't quite reason my way out of it, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It's... well, getting to sleep is a bit uneven to begin with. But then I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. Once I'm awake, my mind just starts going over all the phone calls, the forms I need to deal with... just wondering if I handled things correctly. Because of all that, by the time morning actually comes, I feel like I've hardly rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration. I can still read my books and keep track of things around the house just fine.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've had sad days over the years, of course, just like anyone else. But never anything quite like this. This is really the first time in my life I've felt this sort of... constant heaviness for weeks and months on end. Before all this started, I was generally pretty content.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your sadness has been much stronger than you would have expected, given what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, losing my brother... it's a terrible thing, obviously. But I don't know if this is how I'm supposed to feel after something like that, or if it's... stronger than it should be. It's just hard to say what's normal in a situation like this.\n\nDoctor: When did your brother die?\n\nPatient: He passed away about eight months ago. That's really when this whole heavy feeling started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless... I'm not sure if worthless is exactly the right word. But I do feel a lot of guilt. I just... I keep going back over my brother’s decline in my head. I keep telling myself that if I had been a better sister, or just more attentive to him, I would have noticed sooner. I feel like I could have prevented some of this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s been... well, I'd say about eight months. It really started right around the time my brother’s health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after your brother’s health and behavior changed?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's right. Everything just seemed to shift right around that time, and I haven't been able to shake this feeling since.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I still set out my watercolor things sometimes, thinking I'll paint. But... I'll just sit there staring at the paper, and eventually I end up putting it all away. The pleasure I used to feel in the color and the light, it just doesn't seem to arrive anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings made it hard for you to manage your usual day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, I still take care of the essentials around the house, like cooking and keeping things clean. But... I have pulled back from a few things. I used to be very involved in a walking group, and I did some mentoring for younger women, but I just can't seem to follow through on those commitments right now. I don't feel like I can be fully present with people the way I used to be, so it's just easier to stay home.\n\nDoctor: Is your brother’s situation still ongoing right now?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure. I haven't really... I don't know exactly where things stand with him right now. I just haven't been asking about it lately, so I really couldn't say for certain.\n\nDoctor: When you feel low, are your thoughts mostly about your brother?\n\nPatient: Yes, they often are. I... I just keep going back over his decline in my head. I tell myself that if I had been a better sister, or maybe just more attentive, I would have noticed what was happening sooner. I really feel like I could have prevented some of this if I had just paid attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things in your life, not just your brother?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I worry about a lot of different things. It's really just... my mind always goes back to him. The rest of my life is fairly quiet right now, so I don't have a lot of other things I'm constantly worrying about. It's just that one main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any change there. I think my appetite has stayed pretty much the same. I'm still eating about as much as I usually do.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about your brother, does the heaviness lift at all?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Most days it just feels like... well, like a gray film has settled over everything. Even when I'm not actively dwelling on him, like if I'm just making tea or looking out at my garden, there's still this quiet heaviness in me. It's just always there in the background, and I can't quite reason my way out of it, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It's... well, getting to sleep is a bit uneven to begin with. But then I almost always wake up around three or four in the morning. Once I'm awake, my mind just starts going over all the phone calls, the forms I need to deal with... just wondering if I handled things correctly. Because of all that, by the time morning actually comes, I feel like I've hardly rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any issues with my concentration. I can still read my books and keep track of things around the house just fine.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've had sad days over the years, of course, just like anyone else. But never anything quite like this. This is really the first time in my life I've felt this sort of... constant heaviness for weeks and months on end. Before all this started, I was generally pretty content.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your sadness has been much stronger than you would have expected, given what happened with your brother?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure. I mean, losing my brother... it's a terrible thing, obviously. But I don't know if this is how I'm supposed to feel after something like that, or if it's... stronger than it should be. It's just hard to say what's normal in a situation like this.\n\nDoctor: When did your brother die?\n\nPatient: He passed away about eight months ago. That's really when this whole heavy feeling started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless... I'm not sure if worthless is exactly the right word. But I do feel a lot of guilt. I just... I keep going back over my brother’s decline in my head. I keep telling myself that if I had been a better sister, or just more attentive to him, I would have noticed sooner. I feel like I could have prevented some of this.\n\nDoctor: Has this depressed feeling continued for more than six months after your brother died?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Like I mentioned, it's been going on for about eight months now. It really all started right around the time his health and behavior changed so suddenly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
